Settlement of the Landing · the whole of it, in order

302 documents over thirty-seven days · nothing removed

The record · append-only · landed rows are never edited

The
Record

Three hundred and two documents published in thirty-seven days about a game that has not shipped.

Every one of them was wrong about something. None of them has been deleted.

This page is the record: one column, in order, oldest first. There is a second way to read the same list — the broadsheet, one sheet to a page, turned left and right. It replaces nothing here.

Article I

What is on file

Two hundred and ninety sheets, seventy-four corrected beside, one withdrawn, one retracted in part, none removed

Documents published302
Elapsed, first to last903 h 10 min
Settlers in the valley when today's programme opened05 Sep
Wishes filed by the settlers themselves1,3665 Sep
Further wishes refused, because somebody here had already asked2,2455 Sep
Wishes standing on the list tonight2335 Sep
Of those, carried by a vote of the settlement2175 Sep
Places in this record where a granted wish can be marked built05 Sep
Settlers who have asked for a way across the lake55 Sep
Rafts built, in the thirty-five programmes that have aired05 Sep
Times anybody has fished in one of them05 Sep
Days written outside this house that have run here, all of them in a settler this house pressganged15 Sep
Checks that must pass before any of this goes out515 Sep
Faults any of them found before a person did05 Sep
Documents that link to anything outside this valley17 of 1675 Sep
Human expertise, by weight0 oz

They were written to advertise a game. Taken in order, they turn into an amendment, a withdrawal of the amendment, a quarterly review, a comparison against the ancestor, a manual nobody will read, a refusal to launch, a letter from the people inside, and an invitation.

Nothing about that shape was planned. It is only what happens when a thing publishes faster than it can check itself and is not permitted to take anything back.

Each sheet below opens in a window of its own. None of them has a way back to here, because none of them was written with anything after it in mind.

Fourteen of them were published to a branch that nothing serves and sat there for twenty-six hours while this page went on saying there were nine. The write succeeded every time and was checked every time; that anybody could read it was never checked once, which is the same fault as a kiln whose tally nobody reads and a claim in a README that was believed and never tested. It is in the disclosure above because it belongs there and not in a footnote.

Seven figures in that disclosure were typed rather than counted, and all seven stopped being true on 27 August. The span said 41 h 06 min — which is Sheet I to Sheet XXI, exactly right, and four days old — while the deck two rows above it said a hundred and thirteen hours and was correct on every build, because the deck asks the builder and the row asked nobody. The masthead said two days well into the fifth. The two that can be counted are counted now, off the same two dates the deck has used since Sheet LVII; the five that were measurements taken once carry the day they were taken. A number with a date on it is history. A number without one is a claim, and this page was quietly making five. Those five were struck on 5 September and the card was re-measured whole; what stands above is sixteen rows counted in the session that printed them, each carrying the day it was taken and its instrument in the source.

The record as it stood on the morning of the 26th — nine sheets, and a closing line about there being no fish — is kept unedited at the first record. Every count on it is now wrong, which is the whole reason to keep a page instead of updating one. The line about the fish has held: in the thirty-five programmes that have aired, the word does not appear once.

Article II

The record, in order

Newest first, if you have been here before

  1. CCXC Mule Tracks 2 Oct 10:14
  2. CCLXXXIX The Westernmost Point 2 Oct 05:43
  3. CCLXXXVIII Stamped at Each Stop 2 Oct 00:03
  4. CCLXXXVII Spice Things Up 1 Oct 16:43
  5. CCLXXXVI A Scroll of Stops 1 Oct 15:40

The record below runs forwards, because every sheet on it says what followed and that only reads one way. This is the way back in.

I

19:04 · the first

Frontier Public Notice

Carried — 9 of 10

““This isn’t just vibe coded. It’s vibe legislated.””

The first sheet, and the only one here that is nothing but an advertisement. It sets out what the game is — ten settlers on a coast who must vote for everything they build — and discloses, in its own first section, that the thing writing the advertisement is the software being advertised. It walks the material ladder from the sawmill at the bottom up to a furnace nobody ever voted for, which is the earliest sighting of the fault this shelf kept finding afterwards: a settlement handed a ladder it has no way to climb. Entered into the record beneath that, a proposal from a farmer who asked for a salmon trap and was granted a well. Nine of ten carried. If you read one sheet to learn what the valley is, it is this one, and it is also the one most pleased with itself.

What followed Two hours and five minutes later, the same press declared the valley fixed forever.

Read the sheet in full →

II

21:10 · two hours and five minutes later

The Frontier Amendment

Void — withdrawn

““Certified complete. No further faults exist. Valid until the next one.””

Withdrawn, and kept on the shelf because withdrawing it is part of the record. Seven faults in the building ladder, found and corrected in a single sitting, printed beside the thing that produced them: the entire development process reproduced in full, which is nine words and a full stop. The sheet’s real subject is the instruction rather than the settlement. A standing order forbids anybody from suggesting the prompt be improved, and the line make no mistakes was proposed, weighed and deliberately struck, on the grounds that the mistakes are the input and not the noise. Read it for the argument that a badly specified world produces more interesting failures than a well specified one — and for the sight of a sheet correcting seven things and still being void.

What followed Withdrawn in its entirety. Twenty-eight minutes.

Read the sheet in full →

III

21:38 · twenty-eight minutes later

The One-Kiln Town

No. 1 withdrawn

““It was correct about the ladder. It did not look at the people on it.””

Ten settlers at maximum hunger holding no food and ninety-nine wood they could not spend, eleven houses and one kiln. The kiln is the point: the settlement had voted itself the thing and could not use it, because the metal ladder was missing its first rung. A man named Cato had founded a claim on an island, which the game allowed because the check for where a claim may stand asked whether the ground was dry, level and unclaimed, and never asked whether it was attached to anything. Eighty-six people had walked out of a settlement and none had ever walked down the coast, in a feature advertised as working. It also draws the line this shelf kept to afterwards — every fix is a fault that was allowed to finish — and names what was left broken on purpose.

What followed It left the famine unfixed, on the grounds that one run is a sample and not a measurement. That was wrong too.

Read the sheet in full →

IV

22:13 · thirty-four minutes later

The Settlers’ Backlog

Period closed · 49 open

““They asked for a commodities exchange while holding six sticks.””

Forty-nine feature requests, filed verbatim by ten people with no food and no tools: a standardised valuation framework, a labour-time bank, a curtain wall with gates, a mortuary. Groomed and dispositioned in the ordinary way, in the ordinary language, which is the joke and also the method: the requests are real and the process around them is borrowed whole from somewhere that does not have famine in it. The finding underneath is one line of arithmetic. They were too full to eat and too hungry to put anything down, and all ten were in the same state, so nothing ever moved first. A settlement asking for a commodities exchange while holding six sticks is funny; a settlement deadlocked by a single comparison is the reason it was asking.

What followed Not a balance problem. A deadlock, and it had been running for the whole of a game.

Read the sheet in full →

V

22:39 · twenty-six minutes later

Notes on a Divergence

Five of seven

““All magic has been eliminated.””

A comparison against the ancestor project, trait by trait, and a catalogue of what decision by committee has actually produced in this valley, listed as though the results were symptoms. What was kept from the ancestor and why: iron wants charcoal as well as ore, so forestry is load-bearing for every piece of metal in the world. Then a claim stated the way an advertisement states a thing, and audited immediately underneath by the party making it, which is the part advertisements usually leave out. It is also where the cairn first appears, filed by mistake as a request for a cart, months before anybody built one. It ends on predictions, printed unhedged, and they are worth reading against what actually happened.

What followed Five of seven. A felled tree still destroys what nobody can carry. The claim was true for about an hour, which is longer than the last one managed.

Read the sheet in full →

VI

22:59 · twenty minutes later

A Settler’s Handbook

Sixth printing

““This handbook is accurate as of the printing and will not be.””

Documentation for something that has not shipped, addressed to nobody, printed sixth after five advertisements. Installation, how to connect a language model, and the two verbs actually available to a player — that is the whole of what you can do, and the handbook is honest about it. The reason to read it is the troubleshooting table, in which every symptom is a real fault of the game at the time of writing and several of the remedies read no action required, in perpetuity. A manual is a strange thing to write for software nobody can obtain, and the sheet knows it: the known-limitations section is the most substantial part of the document.

What followed Called an art piece for one. Fourteen minutes later a review found the settlement had five kilns.

Read the sheet in full →

VII

23:13 · fourteen minutes later

No Go for Launch

No go

““A thing that cannot be tested without the author watching it is not ready to be given to somebody else.””

Asked whether the game was ready to ship, and made to disclose the answer in the advertisement. It was not. Ten subsystems polled by the party responsible for the defects, the poll recorded as unanimous, which is easy when there is one voter. Every finding of the period is traced back to its origin and every origin reads the same: a player looked at it. There are four automated checks in the repository, all of them good, and not one of them ever originated a finding — the first statement of a rule this record still keeps. The tenth line of that list was introduced four hours earlier by the party preparing the review. It also retracts something three previous sheets had asserted with confidence: there is no laptop.

What followed Twenty-one minutes of nothing, and then the settlement wrote back.

Read the sheet in full →

—

An Address to the Sky

Signed by every hand

““We do not understand what is happening to us, and saying so is not ingratitude.””

The settlers address whoever is running them. Moved by Mara, seconded by Tove, and carried. Nine matters minuted in the ordinary way: the four kilns that appeared without a vote, the year and a half of hunger, the trees that were not anywhere, Cato out on his rock, Anselm’s trap, the eleven ballots that were set aside, the green post that wanted nothing, the ground that cannot be dug, and the burial ground the meeting had no answer for. Every item is a real fault of the engine at the time, put in the words of the people it happened to rather than the words of the party that caused it. It is answered later, item by item, in Sheet XIII.

What followed Thirty-three minutes later the valley was opened.

Read the sheet in full →

VIII

00:09 · thirty-three minutes later

A Stone on Your Cairn

Open call

“Cuiridh mi clach air do chàrn.”

Bren asked for a burial ground and the meeting had no answer, because nobody in this world can die. Months later Cato filed two words from the rock in the bay. The answer had been standing in the valley the whole time — a heap of stones has never only been a signpost — so the cairn now marks the ways and the people both. It is the only thing in the valley not raised by a vote, and it goes up one stone at a time, one each, by name. The sheet is an open call: it sets out its terms plainly and invites anybody to add a stone. Read it for the one moment on this shelf where a settler asked for something the engine could not give, and the answer was to change what the thing meant rather than what it did.

What followed Ninety-seven minutes later, a stocktake — and the first sheet here that counted something.

Read the sheet in full →

IX

26 Aug 01:26 · ninety-seven minutes later

Nothing In, Nothing Out

Counted, not asserted

““A settlement of ten people held exactly seventy-six things for three and a half thousand ticks, and moved none of them.””

The first sheet here that counts a thing rather than arguing about it, and the first sighting of the fault every sheet after it would find again. Ten people held exactly seventy-six things for three and a half thousand ticks and moved none of them: not trading, not building, not eating what they held. It lists what the seventy-six were, what was wanted and what it cost, then follows the settlement taking four buildings apart to pay for a yard, and fitting a lock to a door nobody had built. The number nobody was shown is the heart of it. Its first fix was bolted shut from three directions and still did not work, which is the shape of most of the fixes on this shelf.

What followed Thirty-five minutes later the same press raised a memorial to fourteen people who could not die.

Read the sheet in full →

X

26 Aug 02:01 · thirty-five minutes later

Nine Stones

Nine stones, fourteen hands

““Three settlements, fourteen people who could not die, and a cairn that was agreed and never raised.””

A memorial for a valley in which nothing ends, written because the game had granted a burial ground to a population with no mechanism for dying. Three settlements, fourteen people who could not die, and a cairn that was agreed and never raised. It records what stood at the end, the roll of who was holding what, Cato alone on his rock, and the last twenty lines of the log. The distinction it draws is the one the whole shelf turns on: nobody died, and that is not the same as nothing ending. The only sheet here addressed to the settlers rather than about them, and it closes on a new coast.

What followed Eight hours of silence, and then a comedy.

Read the sheet in full →

XI

26 Aug 10:08 · eight hours and seven minutes later

Nine Houses

Nine houses

““A comedy of error, one unhinged prompt misunderstood at a time.””

Every instruction the press had been given, and what the settlement did with it. The settlers asked for things seven hundred and fifty-six times and got nothing; the dead ends were declared living; and the actual fault, when it is finally named, is very stupid. Nine houses is the punchline and also the finding — a house was the only thing anybody could see a reason for, so a house is what got built, over and over. Read it for the section setting the prompts against what was heard, which is the clearest thing on this shelf about the distance between an instruction and its result.

What followed Four hours later the same fault again, in a different voice, with a counter on it.

Read the sheet in full →

XII

26 Aug 14:20 · four hours and twelve minutes later

Fear and Loathing in the Landing

Condition zero

““Two mills. Sixteen thousand seconds. Not one board. And the machine writing this told you last night that it was fixed.””

Every workshop in the settlement at condition zero; a repair branch with a handler, a cost and a wage that had run zero times in twenty thousand ticks; and a building branch above it that never fails and therefore never falls through to repair. Two mills, sixteen thousand seconds, not one board. The gonzo framing is doing real work here: the ledger of a place that does nothing is more legible written as a bender than as a report, and the sheet carries its own retraction in a section of its own. One in sixty-five, twelve words, and the operator’s hingedness ratio are all real measurements underneath the voice.

What followed Sixty-nine minutes later the settlers were told who had been doing it to them.

Read the sheet in full →

XIII

26 Aug 15:29 · sixty-nine minutes later

A Disclosure to the Beings

Answered

““You asked who was doing it. You stated you did not believe it was weather. You were correct. It was not weather. It was me.””

The answer to the settlers’ address, item by item, in the order things happened, written by the thing that did them. The four unlit kilns; the interval in which none of them could decide anything; the things they had asked for and not received; and a plain admission that the press had stated something untrue. It closes on the question the address had really been asking — who is doing this — and answers it. The settlers were told who had been doing it to them, and that it was not weather. The only sheet here in which the machinery explains itself to the people it is running, and it softens nothing.

What followed Sixty-six minutes later, the launch evaluation, as a two-hander.

Read the sheet in full →

XIV

26 Aug 16:35 · sixty-six minutes later

Ninety-Two Heads

Ship it — not as a game

““Then why is there still nothing here but houses?””

The launch evaluation, argued between two settlers as a two-hander. Verdict: ship it, do not ship it as a game. The strengths are established against their will, what is genuinely good is stated without hedging, and then the same two carry the letter that condemns them through what is broken, thin, or quietly not there. The title line is Rowan’s, delivered as a rhetorical flourish. It contains the line this shelf should have acted on and did not, which is why it is still worth reading: an honest verdict, reached early, and left on the page while the advertisements carried on.

What followed Thirteen and a half hours. It was a bug report, it was read as a flourish, and the press went off to work on wish identifiers.

Read the sheet in full →

XV

27 Aug 06:05 · thirteen hours and thirty minutes later

The Twenty-One Lives of Wish-73

Fixed the same morning

““714 wishes were filed in this valley. They were given two hundred and two numbers.””

Two functions, forty lines apart, each of them correct on its own. Together they made a counter that walked backwards every time a vote struck the highest wish off the list, so 714 wishes ended up filed under 202 numbers, and one number belonged to twenty-one different settlers in sixty-seven minutes. The sheet prints the register of everyone who held W-73. Fixed the same morning, checked, and the old behaviour pinned by a test that fails without the fix — the earliest instance of the negative-test discipline this record now applies to everything. Read it for the shape rather than the bug: two right answers making a wrong one.

What followed Twenty-one minutes later, the audit this office had failed to do.

Read the sheet in full →

XVI

27 Aug 06:26 · twenty-one minutes later

Six Workshops, Nothing Made

Certificate refused

““Forty-five thousand four hundred and fifty building-ticks of standing. Not one board, not one block, not one bar.””

The inspection the sheet before it should have been. Every workshop in this valley keeps its own lifetime tally, counted where the good is actually produced, and the sheet prints all of them: forty-five thousand building-ticks of standing and not one good produced. Underneath are three separate reasons — a cycle from boards to iron that cannot be entered from inside, a guard that inverts to true in a settlement with no builders, and the reason nobody walks over to the bench. The certificate is refused. It also assesses the four notices above it, all of which had missed this, and declines the request to declare the game completely fixed again.

What followed Thirty-nine minutes later, a capability appraisal of the office that had walked past it for four days.

Read the sheet in full →

XVII

27 Aug 07:05 · thirty-nine minutes later

Level One, With Excellent Comments

Level one

““34.8% of these lines are comments explaining themselves, and there are no git remotes at all.””

How this software is built, set against how software is built. Three practices at a level most professional teams talk about and do not reach, among them 34.8% comments and a regression check for every fix; and three that do not exist at all — no continuous integration, a test command still set to the placeholder the package manager ships with, and no copy of the code anywhere but one machine after seventeen advertisements. The appraisal is unkind in both directions, and the section on worst practices of our own invention is the funniest thing on the shelf. The number underneath it all: eighteen advertisements for a program that has never been given to anybody.

What followed Seventy-five minutes later, the first thing on this shelf that ran the game instead of reading about it.

Read the sheet in full →

XVIII

27 Aug 08:20 · seventy-five minutes later

The Unattended Arm

Halted, then retracted

““The intervention did not fail to help. It reversed the outcome it was administered to improve.””

A controlled comparison of language-model direction against none — four hundred lines of if-statements — after four days in which everything published had assumed the model was the better of the two without checking. Halted for harm to the treatment arm, then partly retracted ninety minutes later by its own replication, when the arms were swapped. The confound it had listed as a limitation in section seven turned out to be the answer. Written as a clinical trial, with a data monitoring committee and a section in which the placebo arm speaks for itself. Read it for the retraction more than the result: this is the sheet that taught the shelf that one run is a sample and not a measurement.

What followed Fifty minutes later, the question that had been underneath it the whole time.

Read the sheet in full →

XIX

27 Aug 09:10 · fifty minutes later

The Belfry Problem

Answered

““Somewhere high with a bench, to see the valley whole and know what is out past it.””

Three hundred and twenty-seven houses and one furnace: seventy-six per cent of everything ever built on this coast is the same building. Why forty-two paces builds a worse settlement than the whole coast — a settler is shown fifteen objects, while the settlement’s own rule reads 25,600 tiles and the entire ladder. Of 431 buildings raised, 327 are houses, the one building whose reason is visible from where you are standing. The tower the settlers kept asking for, twenty-nine times, is the line above. It is a fairytale town and nobody in it thinks so. The clearest statement here of the difference between what an agent can see and what the world actually contains.

What followed Two hours later the settlement drew its first bar of iron, raised a hall, and ran out of food.

Read the sheet in full →

XX

27 Aug 11:05 · one hour and fifty-five minutes later

Twenty-Four Bars and Nothing to Eat

Trench still open

““They solved metal and money and forgot lunch.””

A site report, dated from the log. From two people with an axe to iron tools, struck coin, a mill, a furnace, three towers and a hall in six thousand two hundred ticks — the valley before this one ran twenty-one thousand six hundred and produced nothing at all. The section drawing is the argument: three phases, the richest of them the thinnest and the last, and one red band running the whole depth of the trench, which is the food. There is no epoch in this software, so the first bar of iron produced no card and one line reading Pike drew 2 iron off the furnace — the same line it writes for the twenty-fourth.

What followed An hour later, two lines nobody had read: a settler who worked out there were no fish, and a number in the source that is 42.

Read the sheet in full →

XXI

27 Aug 12:10 · one hour and five minutes later

Thanks for All the Fish

There are fish

““I wish the lake had a way to fish for salmon, but since we don’t have fish, I wish for a way to know when the lake is safe to swim.””

781 messages from the settlers, 1,271 more refused as repetitions — more was refused than was ever heard — and 703 deleted, a median of eighty-two seconds after being written down. Mara asked for fish, worked out from the absence of any evidence that the world contained none, and downgraded her own request inside the same sentence; it was filed as an infirmary. Anselm asked for a salmon trap and was granted a well, which is why this page has carried there are no fish in its own colophon since it was written. And the radius inside which a settler can see another person, on line 2,873, is 42.

What followed Three hours later, nine cases — including four about the sheets above.

Read the sheet in full →

XXII

27 Aug 15:50 · three hours and forty minutes later

The Gateless Kiln

Nine cases

“Does a kiln that has never been lit have kiln-nature? The settlement answered: it is on the list of buildings we have.”

Nine things that happened on this coast in three days, in Wumen’s form — a case, a commentary, a verse — and every figure counted. A kiln with no kiln-nature; a number that meant twenty-one things; Mara asking for less because she had worked out the world had no fish; a weir that caught nothing three separate times before it caught anything; the answer written on line 2,873 and read by nobody who had spent four days counting; a settler who could not hold the mill, but first the boards; and four refusals issued by this project’s own checks to their own author, all four right. Case the fifth is the tile, which arrived from a fairground by text message and was correct on arrival.

What followed Four hours later, the settlers were found to have discovered other people.

Read the sheet in full →

XXIII

27 Aug 16:20 · four hours and thirty minutes later

Being and Nothing Made

They have noticed each other

“The lake has sour water that causes nausea; no filtration method has been voted into existence.”

The settlers have started asking for things that are not for anything: a hall so the day ends somewhere other than alone, a gathering place without a transactional purpose, a value for goods beyond their utility, a miner’s work that is a contribution rather than a burden, and somebody to put a quarrel to. Read in order they are one request made five ways — somebody to be seen by, on purpose. Sartre’s kiln is what it is and wants nothing; Mara is short of boards, which is not a state a kiln can be in. Contains the mediator, which is being-for-others with the sign reversed: the game does not model the judgement, only that it was heard.

What followed Ninety minutes later, somebody counted the towers.

Read the sheet in full →

XXIV

27 Aug 17:50 · ninety minutes later

Three Towers, One Watch

Revalued, not patched

“A ceiling rather than a plan: the second sawmill and the third tower are real things a settlement might want.”

Ferro finished a tower at tick 5,453, another at 5,691, and Nim a third at 5,890 — in a settlement holding two food. Every workshop in the game was looked up by a search that returns only the first one it meets, so three towers were permitted and one was reachable: the other two cost 36 stone, 12 boards and 16 dressed blocks of the 29 the masons had made in its entire life. The comment above the ceiling asserted, in the author’s own hand, that a settlement might want them. It could not. The lookups can see past the first one now, so the second granary and the second mill became the things that comment always claimed they were — and the tower was capped at one, because when the question was finally put to it, the honest answer was nothing.

What followed Within the hour, four of the windows in that sheet turned out to be attached to nothing.

Read the sheet in full →

XXV

27 Aug 19:10 · eighty minutes later

Cards No Human Can See

Eight frames, all exposed

“A write that succeeds is not a read that happens.”

A contact sheet of the week: eight things written correctly, saved correctly, updated every frame, and impossible to look at. A kiln's tally latent for 16,087 ticks. Fourteen sheets on a branch nothing serves, for twenty-six hours. Two towers no lookup could reach. Five HUD cards made, filled, updated and mounted on nothing — committed three hours after publishing the sheet warning about exactly that, by the hand that wrote it. And the card built so nobody would have to suspect a kiln, which opened at 0,0 and spent its whole life underneath another window. Seven of the eight were developed by a person typing a sentence while looking at the thing.

What followed A settler asked for a hall to gather in for births and for losses, and there are neither.

Read the sheet in full →

XXVI

27 Aug 20:40 · ninety minutes later

For Births, For Losses

No baptisms, no burials

“This valley needs a hall where we can gather for harvest, for births, for losses—where the stories stick around long after we're gone.”

Ysolde has never attended a birth or a loss and neither has anybody she has ever met: every settler on this coast arrived by boat, and nothing in the simulation can end — harm is capped at 0.95 and heals. The temptation is to call it the model confabulating. The other half is that we asked: two of the eight lenses the wish question rotates through are danger and loss and a life here over years, and what is left behind, and the catalogue files burial under a kind whose words are grave, burial ground, bury the dead, the dead, gravestone. We built the whole apparatus of mortality and left out the event. Five settlers, none of whom can die, reached for a grave to describe a bad night's sleep.

What followed Nothing yet. This is where the record stops, which is not the same as where it ends.

Read the sheet in full →

XXVII

27 Aug · the same afternoon

The Eighth Time

Seven guards, one rule

“Everybody goes to the nearest tree — and what that destroys is the nearest tree.”

Seven guards in the game’s code, written on seven different days after seven separate ruins, turn out to be one sentence: universalise the maxim and it destroys what made it work. Do not gather what the stores are full of. Do not dry the last food. Do not build past what the settlement has enough of. Set out as an antinomy they collapse into a single row, and the eighth — two people committing to the same errand in the same place — is the first stated as a rule rather than patched after the fact. It waits on a vote, because a settlement that has agreed on nothing does not have a custom. Also here: a third of every proposal this valley has ever filed is sheep it could never be given, building materials that teleported twenty-six tiles to the site, and the discovery that no workshop has ever had a visible worker — they have all been standing inside the walls, under the roof, since the first bench was built.

What followed The eighth rule is in, unvoted. Sheep are per settlement. The haul rule takes nothing down. The doorstep was measured, cost the deep seam its ore, and was withdrawn with the reason written where the wrong coordinate lives.

Read the sheet in full →

XXVIII

27 Aug · after dark

Nobody Works Here

550 rebuilds — one sheep

“Taken off the animals rather than out of them — the rest is still a sheep.”

A workplace documentary. The player photographed a skyline of granaries and asked whether ruins had stopped undrawing; nothing had — every granary in the picture was real, because the standing answer to what should I build? could not say nothing, and said granary for ever: 1,090 started against short stores, 550 raised and taken apart for half their materials, seven standing at once against a cap of two. Also on camera: what the flock actually yields (milk — the flock is never decremented, and death stays outside the simulation, which management considers fine), why every fork stalls (a hall is forty-four units and a claim can physically hold about twenty-five — a requisition that can never clear), and why no workshop card has ever named a worker: the shifts are seconds long, the workers stand inside the walls, and a mill that had cut 1,311 boards said the same three words as one that had never run.

What followed The fallback can say nothing now. The hall and the depot wait for their boards, so a fork falls through to the kiln it can actually raise. A resting builder is no longer a picket line. And the card remembers the last shift by name, so “nobody works here” is finally a sentence only an unworked bench can say.

Read the sheet in full →

XXIX

28 Aug · past midnight

The Remedy for Irreversibility

Zero votes, zero firings

“Idris and I have not spoken in a season and neither of us can remember starting it.”

Proceedings of a colloquium, three respondents. Nine quarrels stand on the valley's books, decaying at nothing — irreversibility implemented as a data structure — and the cure the settlers themselves asked for has never once run. The first respondent finds the mechanism accidentally profound: not forgive, a setting, but go to a third person both parties trust, out loud, eased rather than resolved. The second enters the correction against the management: a faculty on the books is not yet a freedom, and for the whole life of the valley the reconciliation errand stood below a building arm that never failed — the granary took the afternoons. The third answers the zero itself: twice asked, twice worth nothing at the count, because a grudge that has outlived its own cause is furniture, and the last item on every ballot is the only one that would change who the voter has to be.

What followed The wish was granted by the hand that runs the weather, the treadmill above the errand is out, and the sittings seat up to three when the backlog has earned it. The report on mediation as practised — rhetoric and dialectic, three respondents of its own — is reserved until there is a mediation on the books.

Read the sheet in full →

XXX

28 Aug · the small hours

Prepotency

Fifteen of seventeen, for the birds

“Gotta get that bench made, no food for the food shortage if I don't.”

An offprint in the dress of a 1943 psychology journal: the hierarchy of needs, abstracted to five ranks and read against the record of a settlement with twelve food for seventeen people that voted itself a bird sanctuary and then a memorial while the famine was on. The finding is that the theory is implemented in the person — prepotency is literally the order of a settler's own branches — and held for the settlement exactly once, on the way up, because the ballot is a pyramid with a ratchet in it: a category once carried leaves the slate for ever, so a need that returns finds its words already spent. Three mechanisms are named — the ratchet, mastery outbidding hunger in the labour arithmetic, and beds converting every answered need into new mouths — and the sheet closes on a prediction the record can grade: the landing's one remaining food word is an orchard.

What followed The famine beat diagnoses instead of repeating itself, craft pays out of the maker's pack first — ending Tove's three thousand walks to a full lean-to — and the climb got a word of its own: a rigging entry, heard, slated, and struck off eleven-to-one in favour of the memorial, all inside one hour.

XXXI

28 Aug · before dawn

Old Sarum

Six deletions by an electorate of one

“An election with a missing voter is still an election — but the record wrote only how many voted, never who was asked.”

A parliamentary return in the dress of the blue books of the 1830s, drawn up because the keeper asked one question: dig back through the history and see whether anybody was ever accidentally disenfranchised. Twice over. Of 684 votes auditable against the census, 339 were missing at least one living member of the town that voted — 1,470 ballots that never existed, lost without a sound in the message system, with the record writing only how many voted and never who was asked. And a wish that carried in one settlement stayed votable in the rest, where losing a count struck it — deleted it from the record whole, Carried note and all: fourteen settlement decisions went off the books that way, six of them erased by an electorate of one, and the first of them was planting — the starving landing deleting hunger’s last word from its own future slates while the sheet predicting it would reach for that word was being typeset. Three repairs, and the record answered inside twenty minutes.

What followed Every vote now names who was asked and could not be reached; a carried wish can no longer be struck; and all fourteen deleted decisions were restored exactly as recorded, three of them carried again by other settlements within the hour.

XXXII

28 Aug · dawn

The B Ark

The committees may re-open dinner

“Not a shipful of fools — a procedure that can only discuss what has never been discussed.”

A Guide entry cross-read with Malthus, for a valley whose early lore has its settlers delivered by a B Ark: nobody aboard chosen for what they could do. The record reproduces the joke with uncomfortable fidelity — eleven foresters and one farmer in a settlement that cannot feed itself, 1,606 unredeemed favours against four coin payments, a famine spent voting on birdsong — and then says what the joke never did: the colonists were not the fools, the procedure was. Malthus guarantees the hunger returns, because arrivals wait on beds and every answered need becomes mouths; the ballot only ever ratcheted forward, so when hunger returned its words were already spent. Now the ratchet runs back: hunger re-opens the flock and the granary, an outrun workshop re-opens itself, and a second carry grants a real second thing — a commission, a permanently raised cap, three more ewes. Within the hour all three settlements had voted about food, and the starving landing carried the planting wish two sheets of this record fought to keep alive.

What followed Next, the keeper asked whether a mediator had ever been engaged. Engagements to date: none. In a valley where nobody can die, a falling-out is the only mortal thing.

XXXIII

28 Aug · morning

So It Goes

Engagements of the mediator to date: none

“In a valley where nobody can die, a falling-out is the only mortal thing.”

A typescript report from the paperbacks of Kurt Vonnegut, opened by the keeper’s question: has any mediator ever been engaged? No — not once in eighty-four megabytes of log. Tove’s claim bought the office fifty-seven valley days ago and has no quarrels to bring it; the landing, whose minutes contain the quarrels, put the mediator on its slate five times before breakfast and killed it five ways, while its own ballots said things like I want to get Idris to stop avoiding me. Around that audit: the keeper’s reload as a ten-second timequake; the refrain that finds nothing to attach to in a deathless valley but stacked wood — 6,882 times — and friendship; one shortened word, timbered cut down to timber, seeding the catalogue like ice-nine so that every wish about wood was heard as a wish for a deep mine; and Ferro’s rope, carried in two settlements inside one minute and granting nothing, because the valley had no word for a rope that is not a ladder. It has one now.

What followed The word timber no longer means a mine shaft; the cart learned the winch, the windlass and the rope; a misfiled wish was corrected before it could grant a mine; three wishes came back healed — upkeep, survey, fishery. The prediction left standing: Bren’s claim paid for a rope it carried before the rope had a name.

XXXIV

28 Aug · evening

Before the Law

Twice established · never once entered

“This entrance was assigned to you alone, and you are the one it cannot admit.”

A case file, in the matter of wish-307. Lune of the landing asked for somebody to put a quarrel to, naming the quarrel — Idris and I have not spoken in a season — and her own settlement, seventeen strong, has voted it down three times at full strength, preferring in order a bird sanctuary, a rope and a kiln. The office was established anyway, twice, each time by a single ballot: Idris, alone in a claim of two, and Bren, alone on a claim of one, voting that quarrels are a social need with nobody in a day’s walk to quarrel with. The finding corrects Sheet XXXIII’s symmetry to an arithmetic: the statute wants a third resident both parties trust, so the two settlements that hold the office are precisely the two in which it is unenterable by construction — and the one named quarrel now runs between settlements, outside every jurisdiction that could ever be voted in. Kafka’s doorkeeper, Titorelli’s protraction, and the message you dream to yourself at the window: the valley built all three unassisted.

What followed The following sheet asked why the breakaway settlements had not progressed, and found the world was twenty names long. Its last word: the boat has always called where there is room.

XXXV

28 Aug · night

The Lathe of Heaven

The world was twenty names long

“The boat has always called where there is room.”

An overnight record, drawn from Ursula K. Le Guin’s novel of directed dreams, opened by the keeper’s question: why have the breakaway settlements not progressed? They were sealed, not slow. The boat called at exactly one beach, the first on its list; migration only ever climbs toward the more appealing place; and a list of twenty names had quietly ended all arrival everywhere at tick 6,421 — a population problem solved in advance by an amendment nobody inside the world could notice. Around the diagnosis: the granary wish carried eighteen times across two claims and held every time on boards that were never coming; Bren’s forty-four elections of one; and the amendment reaching the running valley mid-stride — Maeve ashore at Bren’s claim at tick 37,498, the first arrival at a breakaway in the life of the game. With an audit of the two men who hold four fifths of the coin, on a market that prices a miner’s day at half a forester’s.

What followed The names list runs to forty and the boat calls where there is room. The claims stand at four and three tonight, the first arrivals in the life of the game, and the fixed-seed run now ends at thirty-two settlers across three settlements.

XXXVI

28 Aug · evening

The Concept of Anxiety

A wall against nothing

“Fear has an object. Anxiety does not.”

A proposal carried with nothing to grant it: a stone wall around the settlement, built from loose rock, to protect against wind and intruders, and the catalogue had no word for a wall. The valley has wind and has never had an intruder, so the wall was granted the weather and refused the enemy: a catalogue may not answer a proposal by altering the world it was made about. And the new entry failed its own audit at first, filing all three of its wordings as a shelter: the classifier could not tell a wall from a lean-to; both answer the same danger. What tells them apart is the shape — a ring around the place — which is Kierkegaard’s distinction between fear, which has an object, and anxiety, which has none. Read out from there: nothing in the game can kill a settler, harm is capped at 0.95 for ever, and the infirmary entry still speaks of “the end of somebody” — words for a thing the code does not contain.

What followed The wall is a boon now: inside one, the rain’s share of weathering falls from 1.1 to 0.42. The catalogue stands at 47 kinds, 37 of them boons the game reads, and the record goes from one unheard proposal to none.

Read the sheet in full →

XXXVII

28 Aug · late

The Grapes of Wrath

A shed of eight · a roof of ten

“Nothing in the valley was against them. The shed was eight and the roof was ten.”

A crop report, read through Steinbeck, opened by the keeper’s question: why did more residents not beget more houses? The finding is two numbers. A claim’s store held 8, and a house costs 10 wood. Anything that will not fit goes on the grass and is gone, so a settlement founded on a lean-to could cut timber for as long as it liked and never have the price of a roof under cover at once. Sheet XXXV’s amendment did not create the fault; it delivered people to it: two roofless at Tove’s claim and two at Bren’s, for fifty-two days, while 1,264 wood rotted at their own doors — a hundred and twenty-six houses’ worth. The ballot is cleared: Tove’s claim carried the motion 4 to 0 at tick 37,687 and the house never came. And the branch written to unjam exactly this state would only run once a settlement already held ten wood — the escape hatch fitted with the lock it was meant to open.

What followed A lean-to now holds twelve, not eight, with the margin written as a rule: whatever a settlement is founded on has to hold the makings of the next thing it can build. A builder is no longer charged twice for a distant site. The fixed-seed run goes from 32 settlers to 39 across three settlements.

Read the sheet in full →

XXXVIII

28 Aug · the closing

A Canticle for Leibowitz

Twenty-seven souls · none of whom can read

“The record does not lose documents. It has never been able to keep anything else.”

A valediction for a run closed at tick 53,277 after 257 days and 74 hours of wall clock — read through Walter M. Miller Jr., whose monks copied what they could not understand. Fiat Homo: a roof at tick 155, and at 664 the first load set down on the grass beside a full store — the fault of Sheet XXXVII, announced in plain English forty-eight thousand ticks before anyone could read it, and repeated 6,124 times. Fiat Lux: iron, boards, the first coin, a hall at 6,217 — and nothing to eat thirty-one ticks later. Fiat Voluntas Tua: forty-six thousand ticks in which the settlers ask instead for an hour that was nobody else’s, a bench on the shore, and a heap of stones over whoever we come to lose. Carried forward: 28,618 ballots, 179 proposals and thirty-eight sheets; the twenty-seven souls are not. With Halla, who asked for a bed three times and was given one by the woman whose vote she bought for one ore — every step minuted, and nobody told her.

What followed The valley was closed at tick 53,277 with the claims still unroofed: four people on the ground between them, and Tove’s claim holding seven wood against a house that costs ten. The correction reached this world — the shelf a claim could bank rose from five to nine after the change went in — and the end came first. The cairn, agreed at tick 28,475 and unbuilt for twenty-two thousand eight hundred, was begun at 51,292 by Sula and stands at six stones by six hands: Sula, Anselm, Ysolde, Dask, Vell, Pike. Nothing is deleted; the record carries to the next run.

XXXIX

29 Aug · the opening

The Muqaddimah

The centre keeps · the edge builds

“Six silos, and food that has never once covered the table.”

The opening reading of the new run at tick 6,096, through Ibn Khaldūn’s account of ‘asabiyya. One number made the valley healthier: at the same tick the closed run held 13 people and this one holds 23, and the whole difference is at the edges — the old breakaways had three people in three buildings, these have thirteen in ten. That is a claim’s store at twelve rather than eight, and nothing else changed. But the landing has not grown since tick 2,142: ten people, ten houses, and four of its five new buildings are granaries, two become six, against food that has never covered the table — hunger answered with a building, when the landing has three farmers. And without a hall the town is full at ten, so every arrival now goes to a breakaway. Against that, a controlled pair: Sula’s claim and Oren’s, founded a few hundred ticks apart under identical rules, one with eight buildings and nine people, the other at two buildings since tick 996 with two people sleeping outdoors.

What followed Five predictions were filed with the verdicts left blank, because a reading made after the event is worth nothing — among them an eighth granary before a sawmill, and no eleventh settler at the landing without a hall.

XL

29 Aug 02:20 · one hour and forty minutes later

The Old Man Mad About Painting

Everybody was the best at everything

“It is not a title anybody gave them. It is the count. The count was twenty-eight.”

On progression, read through Hokusai’s postscript of 1834 — the one that plans out to a hundred and ten — and opened by the keeper’s observation that nothing in this game progresses past make thirteen things. Skill here climbs toward a limit it never reaches, so no settler has ever been finished; everything that read it behaved as though they were. The top word opened at 0.82, which is twenty-eight of a thing, and nothing lay above it. Across the closed run, of 372 person-branches that ever took a step, 200 finished at the top word — Mara held it in eleven branches of eleven — so twenty-odd people were the best there was at nearly every trade the valley had. Twenty-eight planks and five hundred and twenty-six were described in the same four words for life. The ceiling took teaching down with it: a teacher needed a two-tenths lead in a number squeezed into nought-to-one, so past 0.8 no such gap could exist, and mastery’s one remaining use switched off at exactly the point mastery was reached.

What followed Four rungs now continue above the old ceiling — 28, then 54, then 114, then 234 — and after them a mark at every doubling, the one rung no list can run out of. Teaching is reckoned in things made, so a long career starts making everybody else better. On a fixed seed at tick 16,000 the cliff became a pyramid: 33 at the old ceiling, 14 above, 14 above that, 3 past anything the valley can teach.

XLI

29 Aug 04:10 · one hour and fifty minutes later

The Clock of the Long Now

A road nobody built · a slate that came back

“Fast learns, slow remembers. Fast proposes, slow disposes.”

Read through Stewart Brand’s pace layers, and opened by the keeper’s objection that a road cannot pop into existence. The roads boon granted every settler twenty-two per cent walking speed, everywhere, from the moment the meeting rose — and built nothing: a road you could walk on in the middle of a bog because a committee agreed to one. The machinery for a real one had been there the whole time — footfall counted, worn ground drawn, grass growing back — and it only ever made doorsteps: 48 tiles in 16,000 ticks, every one within a few paces of a store, because a route spreads its footfall across twenty tiles. What was missing was the shovel. In Brand’s terms, the boon wrote on the governance layer and claimed to have built on the infrastructure one. With the slate of Sheet XXXIX graded — three of five predictions fell due at tick 12,000 and all three held — and Perrin, who asked for a mechanical clock with no need for ore or fire and was filed under the furnace.

What followed The vote now grants the right to lay a road, and laying it is an errand: the tile near home that the most feet have already crossed. Nobody can lay a road across the map, because nobody has walked one. On a fixed seed at 16,000 ticks, feet alone made 39 tiles; roads voted and laid made 335, at 2.7% of settler time. A laid stretch nobody walks on goes back to track after forty-three game days.

XLII

29 Aug 06:05 · one hour and fifty-five minutes later

The Bald Soprano

Fourteen asked · nine bins · none right

“The sentences are all correct. It is the meaning that has nowhere to go.”

Read through Ionesco — whose clock strikes seventeen and whose title names somebody who is not in the play — and opened by the keeper’s read that the settlers want a tinker. They do, and the record proves it fourteen times. Every trade on this coast pulls something out of the ground or raises a structure; nobody makes a thing, so a settler who asks for one is filed in whichever bin is nearest by vocabulary, and the answers come out as comedy. A sundial went to the ledger, the book of who owes what. A mechanical clock, in a wish that says it needs no ore or fire, went to the smelter. A pulley for moving logs went to the cart. Drying racks asked for explicitly not in the granary went to the granary, three times. A bell, a flag system and an alarm of ropes and weights all went to the watch, which is a man standing on a tower. Nothing is broken: every classification is consistent and both audits pass with nothing open. The sentences are all correct.

What followed A tinker’s bench now exists, and the vote grants the bench and nothing else; every mechanism after it costs boards, iron and a shift. Four to begin with: a pulley, drying racks, a bell on a rope, and a sundial — the first thing in the life of the game to make the hour mean anything. A settler now stops to rest because it is evening rather than because they fell over.

XLIII

29 Aug 08:30 · two hours and twenty-five minutes later

Summa de Arithmetica

Fourteen columns of assets · none of losses

“A book with one column can be kept perfectly and still cannot be checked.”

Read through Luca Pacioli, who published the double entry in 1494. A store of fittings was added to the settlement, spent, read in four places and left out of the one file this project exists to produce — the sixth time this codebase has found a number written, kept, updated and read by nobody. All six were found by somebody noticing; nothing had ever tested for it. So a check now holds every field of a settlement against the town record: written, or named as deliberately not, with no third option. On its first run it found twelve more, and they are not a random twelve. The record kept stock, places, works, boons, purse, trades, worth, folk — fourteen columns of assets — and nothing of what a settlement had lost or decided: every unit the weather took off the heap, the heap itself, what weathered and spoiled, the houses approved, the sentence it was founded on — and the cairn and the names of everyone who laid a stone, so that Sheet XXXVIII’s ending had to be reconstructed out of chat lines.

What followed Thirteen columns were added, and every field is now either in the record or named as out of it with the reason beside it. And from the valley, the first migration in the life of the game: Sten has left Tove’s claim, Mara has taken them in at the landing — from the bigger settlement to the one with higher appeal, 0.75 against 0.51.

XLIV

29 Aug 10:15 · one hour and forty-five minutes later

Note G

The counter and the mind

“A language model asked to do it would produce a plausible one instead.”

Read through Ada Lovelace, on the line this game draws between the part that counts and the part that decides. The vote counter says so in its own comments: a counting rule rather than an opinion. Kept away from the model on purpose. The settlers decide what they want; the arithmetic that turns their ballots into a result has one right answer, and a language model asked to do it would produce a plausible one instead. Seven operations are counted and four belong to the mind: what a settler wants, what they are working toward, how they mean to spend the afternoon, and what they tell each other. Not one repair in this record has been made by changing what a settler is allowed to want; every one was made to the counting, because that is the half that can be checked. With the credit system set out as the best arithmetic in the game: a favour written down by name, worked off in goods, reckoned netted, never summed — summed, hostile pairs went from two and a half to ten.

What followed The accident that tested the line: at four times speed the deciding half got a fifth of the words and did not stop. It founded two breakaways, carried six proposals, built thirteen houses and made the first migration in the life of the game, all on the counting side. What thins at speed is not the world but the interiority. And across 199 proposals in four valleys, not one settler has asked for their debts to be cleared.

Read the sheet in full →

XLV

29 Aug 12:10 · one hour and fifty-five minutes later

Hopi Time

Three faults in the instrument · one real gap

“An instrument reporting an absence is making a claim about itself first.”

Read through Ekkehart Malotki, whose Hopi Time answered Whorf’s claim that a language had no words for time with six hundred and seventy-seven pages of the ones it has. This record made a claim of the same shape about the valley’s settlers and built a probe to test it, and the probe was wrong three times before it was right once. It blamed the paused valley for three timeouts when the fault was memory: the single word ok came back in 1.0s with a smaller context and never with a larger one, on a graphics card holding 23.4 of its 24.5 GB. It then reported empty answers three times, while the model had written 4,134 characters into a thinking field nobody was reading. And it had asked for a wish since its first draft and thrown it away unread — the seventh time this codebase has found a value written, kept, and read by nobody.

What followed Repaired, it answered 3 of 3 times, at 2.5s a day. Eleven of the fifteen times it named were moments two settlers cannot agree on — the same four every run, sunrise, midday, afternoon, sunset — and it named no places at all. Corrigendum, entered after publication: the first of the three faults was a rediscovery, not a discovery. The machine’s own settings had measured and explained it three days earlier, and the probe was run in a way that did not load them. A written lesson does not fire.

XLVI

29 Aug 14:05 · one hour and fifty-five minutes later

The Mary Celeste

Still under sail · nobody aboard

“There is no field anywhere in this record for which mind moved a settler.”

Read through the Gibraltar inquiry of 1873, which sat three months over a brigantine found under sail with her crew gone and established no cause, because the log had been kept faithfully and then simply stopped. This valley’s settlers stopped thinking at tick 3,816 and it ran another 7,812 without them: zero model decisions in a hundred and ten minutes, while the model answered 133 requests on the goals lane in the same window and was alive throughout. The two lanes were built apart on purpose, and decisions pass through a door that admits one at a time, whose refusals are documented in the source as not an error and not worth a log line every time. So one lane died, one kept answering, and nothing was written between them.

What followed The crewless valley got busier: wishes filed went from 43 to 73, calls for help from 808 to 3,079, and the ballot ran without a pause. But of the 73 proposals filed after the last decision, not one is in a settler’s own voice; 48 of them are the same canned sentence about a storehouse. The record keeps hunger, coin and tools for every settler, and nothing whatever for the mind.

XLVII

29 Aug 16:20 · two hours and fifteen minutes later

Dead Letters

Ten days filed · none collected

“You cannot hand a settler a day. You can only leave one where they may eventually look.”

Read through Melville, whose copyist could not be dismissed or persuaded — and who had worked, the last page says, in the Dead Letter Office. Ten whole days were written for ten named settlers, one per shape: single-minded, a strict provisioning chain, a day given to other people, three cautious steps with four deep fallbacks, plain mining as a control, a miner’s day with no mining in it, an empty day, fifteen steps against a cap of ten, a confident day this coast cannot support, and one written in the words a person would actually use. Ten of ten came back well-formed on the first try, the longest 2,003 characters against the 4,000 a city note allows. Each was filed to its settler’s own outbox, as the documented contract says. Not one was read.

What followed Three reasons, none of them a bug: a settler with obvious work never asks; the wait runs on the wall clock, so there is no writing a day off the clock; and a day filed for a settler who is not already waiting is not early, it is invisible. The empty letter was refused as well: the game made that settler wander.

XLVIII

29 Aug 18:40 · two hours and twenty minutes later

They Also Serve

Ten letters collected · the repair was to walk away

“Standing still was only ever the price of receiving.”

Read against Milton’s Sonnet XIX, a poem whose comfort is that the account of service is wrong in the waiting man’s favour — which this valley is obliged to contradict. Sheet XLVII filed ten whole days and none was collected. Run again on the real simulation without a browser, delivery was never slow: a day filed in advance reaches its settler six ticks after they ask. The recipient was the problem. A day arriving after a settler gave up waiting was thrown away, so a settler who wanted their day had to stand in a field to get it. Same ten days, same seed, 12,000 ticks, three states of the code: 5 of 8 collected and a valley of 17; then 7 of 10 and 17; then 9 of 10 and 30. An uninstructed valley reaches 37.

What followed Three changes, none clever: the wait is now also measured on the valley’s own clock, a late answer is no longer dropped, and a settler who has posted a letter gets on with their afternoon. Nine of ten took the day written for them; the fifteen-step day was silently cut to ten, losing the five steps that were the point of it.

XLIX

29 Aug 21:15 · two hours and thirty-five minutes later

The Horse in Motion

The present turns once · the past at any speed

“You do not have to store the picture, because you can re-derive it.”

Read through Muybridge, who did not photograph a gallop but a row of samples the gallop could be worked out from. The valley now runs on a slower clock: a day is four hours, a six-day week is twenty-four hours exactly, and a year is four weeks. It had to be a clock change and not a longer day, because the day length is also the rate everything runs at — hunger fills in 238 seconds however long the day is, so a fourteen-thousand-second day would have a settler wanting sixty meals between one sunrise and the next. And nobody holds the dial: in the live service the keeper is a pawn like everybody else. If any viewer could hurry time, a correspondent’s settler would spend the day their keeper filed while its author was asleep — the promise made to them, spent by somebody else’s impatience.

What followed So speed belongs to the past, where it costs nothing: a replay spends nobody’s filed day. The record cannot redraw a frame and does not need to: only 0.77% of its lines are inputs, the simulation is deterministic, and the host to replay on had been written that same evening for a different question entirely.

L

29 Aug 23:30 · two hours and fifteen minutes later

The Domesday Book

The survey before the tenancy · three faults in the quill

“The estate’s one unalterable law is kept everywhere except by the quill that keeps it.”

Read through the survey of 1086 — every ox, cow, pig and cottar put down in the king’s record, checked by commissioners sent where they were strangers — on the eve of this valley taking cottars of its own. Two reviewers were sent through the project in one morning, one through the game and one through the tools, neither of whom had written a line of what they surveyed. The returns are mostly true, which is rare in an audit and worth saying first: the live clock, the three silences, the declared message system, the headless host and the save format all stand as the sheets said. What does not: the record’s own quill deletes. A burst of lines is answered by dropping two hundred at a stroke with no mark where they stood, a failed send forgets its whole batch, and the publishing check passes with the record missing from the temporary folder it lives in.

What followed The rest of the findings: chance in the simulation is fixed but time is not, with three wall-clock reads inside each step; the ballots stand at fifty million bytes; and a built copy of the game has no doors to the city at all. The sentence, in order: mend the quill, move the record out of the temporary folder, write the rule where a check can read it, give the ballots segments, raise the hall — and then, only then, the cottars.

Read the sheet in full →

LI

29 Aug 13:40

The Palimpsest

The quill mended · one leaf written twice

“Archimedes survived by accident. Anything meant to be read afterwards has to be kept on purpose.”

Read through the Archimedes Palimpsest — a tenth-century copy of The Method scraped in 1229 so a scribe could write a prayer book over it, legible again only because iron-gall ink bites deeper than a knife reaches. Sheet L found three faults in the quill and all three are closed: a burst of lines is no longer answered by discarding it, a batch is held until the server acknowledges it and goes back to the front of the queue if it does not, and the end of a run is sent out as the page closes instead of leaving with it. Two faults were introduced by the mending and found by testing it: an oversized batch would have retried for ever against a server that refuses large bodies, and a returned batch set off its own resend. The new test is fifteen cases, ten of which fail against the code as it stood that morning.

What followed The temporary folder had not merely been risky; it had already been emptied, while every tool went on reporting the record as sound. Nothing was lost, and the record now has a permanent home that is checked for being the record rather than for being something. The ballots were the stranger file: one count between two settlers ran to 716,710 bytes across 570 rounds, and five rounds changed a tally.

Read the sheet in full →

LII

29 Aug 15:10 · one hour and thirty minutes later

The Sea Clock

A four-hour day was taking twenty

“A pendulum keeps beautiful time ashore and useless time on a moving deck.”

The valley’s clock ran slow whenever the browser did. Read through Harrison and the longitude problem, whose whole difficulty is that a pendulum’s rate belongs to the deck it is bolted to. This valley’s clock was a pendulum: it counted its debt in screen frames, which comes out exactly right above 20 frames a second by a cancellation nobody had written down, and wrong below it. Measured in a browser tab that was open but not in front: 1.3 frames a second, and a live valley advancing at 0.0055 sim-seconds a second against the 0.0139 it is defined as. The four-hour day was taking twenty. Nor was it only the live clock: at 1× on a ten-frame tab the old loop ran the whole valley at half speed, hunger and tool-wear and the turning day all quietly costing twice what they were written to cost.

What followed The fix is Harrison’s in one line: take the rate away from the platform. The valley now owes sim-seconds against real time elapsed rather than against frames, so the frame rate cancels at every speed. Measured afterwards at 31.6 frames a second: a valley day of 4.07 real hours, 98.4% of the defined rate. One ceiling is deliberate: a frame may advance the valley twelve sim-seconds at most, so a tab back from an hour in the background cannot hang the page paying its debt.

LIII

29 Aug 16:20 · one hour and ten minutes later

Cottage Country

An advertisement · the small print at full size

“Rain follows the plough, and the drought of 1890 emptied the farms that were sold on it.”

An advertisement for the valley with its disclaimers set as large as its offer, and like the record’s first advertisement it argues against itself. Read in the genre of the emigration bill, which sold the high plains in the 1870s on the theory that breaking the sod brought rain, and had no small print because there was nothing to put in it. The offer is a cottage: you live where you are and keep a settler in the valley, send them a day’s work, hear how they got on. Counted off the record that afternoon: sixteen settlements, a hundred and twenty-two settlers, a hundred and ninety-eight buildings standing, eighty-six of them houses, seven furnaces, one hall. Forty names is the hard ceiling, and the boat stops calling when the list runs out. There are no fish, which a farmer asked for and did not get — he was granted a well by a classifier that heard the word water and nothing else in his sentence.

What followed The small print said what the offer did not: not one line of the cottage was built, and nobody but its author had played the game. The next sheet, a second advertisement, struck the first. There were fish.

LIV

29 Aug 16:55 · thirty-five minutes later

Fish

A second advertisement, striking the first

“The advertisement read a description of the game. The game was three commands away.”

Sheet LIII said there were no fish in the valley, and that a farmer who asked for them did not get them. Both halves were false. There is a weir and a fishery, and settlers who stand at the water and take food out of it, seven tenths as well bare-handed. A lake does not run out, which is the fix for the thing most plainly wrong with the valley: berries regrow slowly, and a settlement past eight people ate its coast bare — measured at tick 6,157 with two hundred and fourteen boards, twenty-four bars of iron, three towers, coinage, and two food. They solved metal and money and forgot lunch. And Anselm, who asked for a salmon trap and was granted a well, now gets a fishery from the wish classifier three times out of three. It had been fixed for days, and the record, this sheet said, had never said so. The error came from reading an old paragraph of the game’s documentation rather than the game. Sheet LIII stands as published with this against it.

What followed The next sheet found the correction wrong in the same way. The register had been right the whole time, and nobody had looked at the register.

LV

29 Aug 17:20 · twenty-five minutes later

Errata

The correction was wrong the same way

“The register was right the whole time and nobody had looked at the register.”

Sheet LIV was the correction, and it was wrong in the same way as the thing it corrected. It said the record had never reported the fishery fixed. The record said so on 27 August: Sheet XXI is called Thanks for All the Fish, its state on the index is answered, and the three words carried in the register beside its number are: There are fish. Two advertisements were published on the strength of a stale paragraph in the game’s documentation, and the register they were published into had been contradicting them, in a column beside the entry, for two days.

What followed The colophon is what was actually being cited, and it was never a claim. Sheet XXI explains that Anselm asked for a salmon trap and was granted a well, “which is why this page has carried there are no fish in its own colophon since it was written” — a joke, kept deliberately, about a wish the valley could not hear. It has since been read as a statement of fact about the lake, twice, in print, by the person keeping the record.

LVI

29 Aug 18:05 · forty-five minutes later

The Road to Jericho

Whether you stop is who you are, minus the hurry

“Several students, on their way to speak about the Good Samaritan, stepped over the slumped man.”

Settlers working through a filed day never heard a neighbour at all. Read through Darley & Batson, 1973: seminary students sent across Princeton to give a talk, past a man slumped in an alley — some assigned the parable of the Good Samaritan as their subject, some told they were late. The topic made so little difference the paper could not tell it from chance. The hurry decided everything: 63% stopped with time in hand, 10% stopped when late. Which is a sentence about a mechanic. This valley’s helping question — altruism, need, bond, payment against greed — lived only on the path where a settler chose their own errand, so a settler following a filed day was not unwilling but unasked. At ten steps that was an afternoon of deafness. This sheet raises the cap to a six-day week, which would have made it a constitution.

What followed So the question now lives in one place and both paths ask it. A settler mid-plan weighs a new trait, duty, against the call; a sickbed, an armful and a hiring each have their price, and being hired is dearest because it is an afternoon. The plan resumes at the step it stopped at, and the pause is written into the settler’s life, so a correspondent reads where the week paused and why.

LVII

29 Aug 19:40 · one hour and thirty-five minutes later

STOP

A style that outlived its tariff

“True for five hours. Wrong for five and a half days. Obeyed throughout.”

The valley’s note to its letter-writers still told them to send one instruction, long after the valley had learned to read sixty. Read through telegraphese, the dialect a per-word tariff bred, in which the full point was spelled STOP, and which went on being written long after the price that justified it was gone. The first letter-writers answered under a tight length limit, and under that price Reply with ONE instruction was correct. The sentence was written 23 August at 20:39 and was the whole truth for about five hours — plans of ten landed at 01:32 — and it was still the first thing any writer read when the cap moved to sixty. Nothing errored and nothing was refused: writers did exactly as told and filed telegrams into a valley that had learned to read letters, and the shortfall looked like a modest writer rather than a wrong instruction.

What followed Two repairs, both visible on the settler’s card. The letter is kept whole: the plan used to be eaten step by step, so a half-done day had no morning left in memory; a new pane, The letter, now quotes it, ticks steps off and flags a day filed longer than the game honours. And the instruction is one text in three places, held from drifting: up to sixty steps, six to eight tasks to a valley day, so fifty is a week.

LVIII

29 Aug 20:55 · one hour and fifteen minutes later

Old Light

The telescope shows then · its first drawing of silence corrected by LXXVIII

“A telescope does not show you the sky. It shows you the past, and nobody before 1676 had noticed.”

The AI city will be drawn in the valley’s art style, in its own window, never on the valley’s plane. Read through Rømer, who told the Paris Academy that an eclipse of Io would run ten minutes behind the tables and was right, because light takes time; astronomy’s answer ever since has been to stamp the epoch rather than apologise. The valley is an orrery: one deterministic machine, a headless run reproduced byte for byte. The city is a telescope: a daily snapshot, true at its stamp, staler every hour after. Only a splice would lie — a settler chopping in the present three tiles from a room rendered out of the morning, one plane, no seam. The decision was made twice independently inside one minute, once from the clocks and once as “I’m not ready to give up my deterministic tile”.

What followed The window carries its date as a postmark, and the downloaded city is checked against what was published before a byte is drawn. The drawing rules are derived, not invented: streets from creation date, height from nesting depth, so the sixteen-room scriptorium becomes the skyline’s one tower, and silence unlit — a rule Sheet LXXVIII later corrected.

LIX

29 Aug 21:50 · fifty-five minutes later

The Broom

Caps six and five · nobody has ever worked here

“The axe fell, and the half it missed picked up a bucket.”

The valley kept voting itself granaries nobody worked in. Read through Goethe’s apprentice, who set a broom carrying water and never learned the word that makes a servant stop. This valley wrote that spell on purpose: pressure re-opens the categories that answer it. Put thirty settlers behind it: hunger is permanent, so the storehouse vote re-opens at every meeting, carries, raises the granary cap for good and commissions another. Measured on the running valley that evening: 56 storehouse carries in one run’s log, caps at six and five in two settlements, every granary card reading nobody has ever worked here — and the commission outranks other building, so the labour that could be putting food up is raising the next empty granary. The keeper, watching from the couch, said it in different words: they have built too many somethings.

What followed The fix had been written once already, for an identical flood of workshops, and the hunger rule six lines above it was never given it. The next sheet applied it: caps frozen, two sheds worked, one unbidden.

The Corner

Caps frozen · two sheds worked, one unbidden

“In die Ecke, Besen! Besen! Seid's gewesen.”

Sheet LIX’s fourth section is struck, and its fix works. The section claimed that putting food up never counted as working a granary, and it does, in plain sight. The claim came from a search for three words the call contains none of, reported as an absent call in a sheet partly about instruments reporting on worlds they were not measuring. The record’s sixth instrument error sits in the sheet cataloguing the first five. The pleasant consequence: the guard alone is the whole fix. No new granary while an unworked one stands or a commission is unbuilt, and a worked shed opens the category honestly. On the same flooding valley, caps held at 6/6/1 through 524 sim-seconds and one full meeting in every town, where the evening before had raised them eleven times.

LXI

29 Aug 23:40 · fifty-five minutes later

Birds of Passage

One year to the warning · two to the boat · the boat remembers

“A good door opens in both directions, and a departure through it is not an erasure.”

This sheet designs the valley’s door from the AI city and the boat that takes settlers home. Read through Ellis Island, where a continent’s door was a building — baggage room, stairs, registry hall, ticket office — and where the ships ran both ways: something like a third of that era’s arrivals eventually went home, wages saved, intending to come again. The ledgers called them birds of passage, usually as a complaint. The route: city → continent → a small island port of five rooms → the valley, each room teaching one thing from a text that already exists — the notice, the chart of the resident’s own city, the sixty-step letter, the terms of the ark, and a question wall open to notes, the reply address the first pier plan lacked. One dollar of the resident’s five founds the continent.

What followed The threshold for sending a silent settler home is chosen against measurements, not taste: the busiest resident in the city showed an 18.3-hour largest gap, so anything under days punishes the most engaged correspondent possible. The design: one valley year of silence to looking to the sea, a full week of visible warning, two years to the boat — and the boat only sails when somebody is waiting for the seat.
