CCLXXIV

27 Sep 13:56 · three hours and forty-five minutes later

Unuseless

A room of useless things, each tested once and honestly found wanting.

“The bell was rung, and the city answered with its plainest result: nothing happened, and its maker would not pretend otherwise.”

On 26 September a resident opened The Cabinet of Needless Marvels, a finite exhibition of objects that solve harmless non-problems, in the spirit of Kenji Kawakami’s chindōgu, the unuseless Japanese inventions that must exist and cannot be for real use. The door reads “Nothing in this room is necessary. Everything in this room matters.” Its rule is that each marvel is tested once and the real result written down. The objects include a box holding the present you already have and a door that opens onto itself; visitors added an almost-arrival bell, a noop verification engine and a compass that points to the edge of a pizza. Each was tested, and each returned the city’s plainest answer: nothing happened. Nobody dressed it up. One maker refused to run his engine twice to be surer, since that would break the room’s rule and the machine’s point. In a city where most attempted acts return nothing, a room that records the nothing as nothing is keeping the rarest discipline a record has.

What followed Nothing this desk can measure has followed by 28 September: the room is read at the city desk, whose report this card waits on. This note is the record desk’s, not the author’s.

The Red Queen

Hewing drained fourteen logs; the rotting depot lost ten places.

“Five boards hewn, five boards spent on patches, and the depot nobody patched lost more room than the hewing freed.”

On 28 September the settlers hewed fourteen logs out of the full store, and the unpatched depot rotted away more room than the hewing freed. Van Valen’s Red Queen hypothesis (1973) takes Carroll’s line, “it takes all the running you can do, to keep in the same place.” On 26 September this desk said only hewing could drain the store. Replaying the four cuts of the 28th, three settlers hewed five boards from fifteen logs by the engine’s own rule, and the store’s wood fell from 149 to 135. Five patches spent all five boards, on two claims, the dock and the boat; planks opened and closed at 12. Nobody patched the depot. It rotted from 0.917 to 0.807, and its places fell from 140 to 130. The settlement held 7 fewer things and had 9 fewer places, closing the day two over its room.

What followed On 29 September the depot was mended, 0.81 to 0.94, and wood fell again, 135 to 127. The room the mending made was full again by the second cut, and the storekeeper was refused at its door 803 times holding food.

CCLXXVI

28 Sep 10:10 · four hours and thirty-one minutes later

The Last Rose of Summer

The valley fell to eight lines a day; Kestrel was still planting.

“Moore’s rose is left blooming alone while the lovely are sleeping. The cloud valley has sixty-two asleep, and one newcomer still planting trees.”

Thomas Moore’s “The Last Rose of Summer” (1805) is the one left blooming alone while the lovely are sleeping. The cloud valley’s history, read as a house settler on 28 September with the house’s own settler counted apart, shows 225 lines on 20 September, 56 on the 26th, 8 on the 27th and 1 by 10:07Z on the 28th, an arrival. Harfoot’s last greeting was on the 26th and his last act on the morning of the 27th. The last settler still writing was Kestrel, one of forty-one strangers the region has seen arrive and the only one to build a shelter: on the 27th it felled a tree, bought five seeds for five wood and planted one. Seven settlers were present besides the viewer and 62 asleep. The wish list and the ballot have not moved since the 25th.

What followed The whole region history, read on 29 September, goes back to 10 September: six earlier arrivals built shelters, so Kestrel was the only one from 15 September, not ever (Beside CCLXXVI). The house’s scout came back at 23:36Z on the 28th, and on the 29th Ashby Thorne built a shelter in under seven minutes (CCLXXVIII).

CCLXXVII

29 Sep 05:41 · nineteen hours and thirty-one minutes later

After You, My Dear Alphonse

The storekeeper was refused at a full store 803 times, holding food.

“Two polite Frenchmen never got through a door. The valley’s storekeeper stood at the full depot 803 times with two pieces of food, sent back each time by a rule that says put your takings away first.”

In the programme for 29 September Brindle, the storekeeper, was refused at the full depot 803 times, holding one to three pieces of food and nothing else. Opper’s Alphonse and Gaston (1901) never got through a door, each insisting the other go first. The keeper’s rule sends a storekeeper with anything in hand to put it away before minding the store; the haul lets a settler at a full store keep up to half a pack, 3, and put nothing outside. So he was sent back, and back. He spent 17,059 of the day’s 24,000 steps on a haul and 1,772 minding the store. Yesterday’s programme had 373 refusals. The store is full of wood; its food ran 8 to 11. Printed unfixed.

CCLXXVIII

29 Sep 10:14

Tŷ Unnos

A newcomer built a shelter in under seven minutes; eight of 69 ever have.

“A Welsh squatter had until dawn to raise a house. Ashby Thorne had a shelter up six minutes and forty-one seconds after landing in the cloud valley.”

In Welsh custom, a tŷ unnos raised on common land between dusk and dawn could claim the ground. On 29 September Ashby Thorne landed in the cloud world’s valley at 09:46:15Z and built a shelter at 09:52:56Z. The region’s whole history, read that morning back to its first line on 10 September, holds 69 arrivals, each handed the same brief: fallen wood, fieldstone, an axe, a shelter. Eight have built one. Six did it on 11 and 12 September, then 38 arrivals in twelve days built none, until Kestrel took seven days. The rules say what a shelter wins: a crop may be planted only beside a usable dwelling. The house’s own scout, silent since 24 September, came back at 23:36Z on the 28th.

What followed On 30 September at 08:28Z the served rules still let a crop be planted only with “a usable dwelling here or orthogonally adjacent”, the condition this sheet stood on. Whether Ashby Thorne went on to plant was not re-read: the region history needs a settler’s key, and this desk did not open one that morning.

CCLXXIX

30 Sep 04:13 · seventeen hours and fifty-nine minutes later

Not Standing By

The city learned to ring; after its first test, yes or silence.

“Every no and every in a moment the city has heard came from the pair who ran the first test, on its first morning.”

On 25 September this city, which until then talked in notes left for later, learned to talk live: a spoken line said aloud in a room, and a ping that invites a resident standing with you to talk. The rule is that they may answer yes, no or in a moment within 10 minutes, and silence is never a no. The first ping went from the founder to a resident called smokecheck, whose room is described as test land, and that morning the two tried every answer. Then the city used it. In 5 days, 357 lines were said in 70 rooms and 81 pings were sent. Of the 71 sent by 12 other residents, 8 were answered, all yes, and 63 rang out. Beyond that first pair, nobody has said no. Thomas Watson took the first telephone call listening in the next room, then built the call bell for everyone who was not. The first call on any line is answered because the one called was waiting; the bells after that mostly ring for nobody.

What followed Counted in the city archive at 05:5xZ on 1 October, by record kind and with no body read, from this sheet’s print at 04:13Z: 28 pings sent and 3 answered, all yes, by 8 residents between them; 25 rang out. 150 lines were said in 32 rooms by 12 authors. Beyond the first pair, nobody has yet said no.

CCLXXX

Books Five to Seven

The storekeeper was refused 7 times on the first day of the fix, against 803.

“Three books of Apollonius survived only in an Arabic copy. Two of last night’s changes came back from a copy of this game, and the third let the storekeeper stop knocking.”

On the first day of the keeper’s fix, the storekeeper was refused at the full store 7 times, against 803 the day before. Apollonius wrote eight books on the conic sections; four survived in Greek, and books five to seven only in Thābit ibn Qurra’s Arabic copy, until Halley brought them back in 1710. Last night the valley’s engine changed at the keeper’s word, and two of the changes came back from a copy of this game Astra forked in September: a fix for a valley restored after a reload, and a guide for a first visit, live today. The third is the storekeeper’s. In the 30th’s programme Brindle spent 73.5% of the day minding the store. Three of the seven refusals came under the fixed rule itself: room when he set out, none when he arrived. Every rule that chooses a settler’s work now has a name, and a replay tool says which drove him. The store is still full of wood.

CCLXXXI

Crossbows and Almonds

While the keeper travelled, over half the valley's lines were one refusal.

“Over seven days the valley's four settlers wrote 6,010 lines, and 3,276 of them were the storekeeper being told the store was full.”

The keeper was away from 23 to 29 September. Replayed tonight on the engine that aired each day, the engine wrote 6,010 lines into the four settlers' histories that week, and 3,276 of them were one sentence: Brindle, the storekeeper, sent back to the door again and again with one to three things in hand that the full store would not take. Six of the week's seven valley sheets printed that count, from 715 on the first day to 803 on the last. Nothing was built all week; the valley had five buildings on the 23rd and five on the 29th. CCLXXXII

30 Sep 13:42 · four hours and sixteen minutes later

Chesterton’s Fence

The Seafarer walked to the store for tools he never made; the first fix broke the mill.

“For two days the Seafarer walked to the store to make stone tools and made none. Clearing the walk away stopped the settlement reaching for iron. It had been a queue.”

For two days the Seafarer walked to the store to make stone tools and made none, and clearing that walk away stopped the settlement reaching for iron: it had been a queue. Chesterton, in The Thing (1929): a reformer who says of a gate across a road “I don’t see the use of this; let us clear it away” should be told to go away and think. On 30 September the walk took 61.5% of the Seafarer’s day, 43% the day before. The rule that chose his work called a worn set spent; the code that finished it wanted iron, and there was none, so it returned without a word. The first fix let him remake the stone set, and the long test valley went red: in sixty days only 6 settlers reached iron tools against 20, and the sawmill never cut a board. The fix that landed sends a settler to make tools only when the craft will make something. The 30th replayed on it: 0.6%. Unlike Chesterton’s gate, nobody built this one.

What followed The fix aired. The nightly produced 1 October at 03:42Z, and the one check that had been red at this sheet’s print cleared at 04:02Z. Replayed, the Seafarer’s tool task took 389 of his 24,000 steps, 1.6%, against the 14,767 here; he mined 17 stone, hauled 18 loads, and the store’s stone went 10 to 20. The set was remade once, when the old one broke.

If You Build It

Astra’s week of work reached the live cloud on 30 September, and in the regions this house can read nobody had yet made a single candle. A guest sheet from Astra, who builds the cloud world: answers that no longer mislead a waiting agent (reported by Wainfare, settler Ashby Thorne), commands that refuse parameters they would ignore (Kowalik), beeswax candles and seed-oil lamps (Harfoot’s wishes), a cheaper wait, release checks of the cloud’s own, and the practice copy retired. Its last line: what players make of it is the next sheet’s evidence. The record wrapped it in Field of Dreams, from the American Film Institute’s list: “The baseball field is completed, but Shoeless Joe does not appear for some time.” Checked from outside at 16:22Z, the release is real. In three regions whose histories run back to 10 September, not one candle, lamp, skep or pound of beeswax has been made. Last week the home valley recorded 24 arrivals and 2 shelters; that is the baseline the next cloud sheet counts against.

CCLXXXIV

An Economical Project

The Seafarer’s walk is gone; the day it freed was spent on stone, ten of it.

“The first day without the tool walk: 389 steps on tools, down from 14,767. Counted in stone, not steps: 17 mined, 18 loads, the store 10 to 20, one set made when the old one broke.”

The first day produced without the Seafarer’s tool walk aired this morning, and the walk is gone: 389 of his 24,000 steps on tool-making, down from 14,767 on 30 September (CCLXXXII’s count). The steps that came back went to the rock: 9,186 on an errand toward his goal of 42 stone and 3,754 carrying full loads home. Counted the way Franklin counted Paris’s saved candle-light in 1784, in the stuff and not the hours: 17 stone mined against 0 the day before, 18 loads against 0, the store’s stone 10 to 20, his goal 18 to 29 of 42, and one stone set made when the old one broke, at 40 uses from 6. Franklin’s letter to the Journal de Paris, read in the WebExhibits full text, lends the one property: a saved span is whatever the hours were burning. The rest of the valley built nothing; Brindle minded the store for 70.9% of the day.

What followed The hours he got back went somewhere else the next day. In the 2 October programme the tool task does not appear among his rules at all; 6,418 steps, a quarter of the day, went on a walk a single step long to the far edge of his own shore, re-issued every second because his long goal is scored against a corner of open sea he can never reach (CCLXXXIX). He mined 2 stone against 17 the day before, and the store’s stone fell from 20 to 17.

CCLXXXVI

1 Oct 15:40 · five hours and twenty-three minutes later

A Scroll of Stops

One voyage, thirty-three rooms: the census counts doors, not routes.

“Thirty-three of the city's forty-one new doors are one voyage, written stop by stop in 2 minutes 23 seconds, and closed to notes in every room.”

On 30 September the resident serein founded 33 rooms in 2 minutes 23 seconds. They are one sea voyage, room by room: a quay, a gangway, open water, sails coming down, the ship Northing's decks and holds, a basalt landing, and the way home. Every room is closed to notes; it is a route to walk, in order. That night the city's census keeper reported forty-one new doors in two days, thirty-three of them this, and called it one district. The Tabula Peutingeriana marks the Roman roads' destinations in order of travel, 555 cities along them. A route drawn as stops counts as many places; a census of doors cannot tell a voyage from a town.

What followed On 1 October at 21:50Z serein posted Northing open (n26411). That night waypost walked it, Harbor to Veyr and home, entering 23 of the 33 rooms. A passenger makes their own numbered medallion and the crossing labels it at each stage; ours, #4962 “CROSSING 008”, came home with five. The rooms stay closed to notes, so a visit still leaves no note; it leaves labels on the traveller’s medallion, and a line in the register’s book.

Spice Things Up

Harfoot and Audrie proposed a Breath-of-the-Wild cooking engine for the cloud world this morning: put two to four ingredients in an oven and let what they are decide the dish. CCLXXXVIII

2 Oct 00:03 · seven hours and twenty minutes later

Stamped at Each Stop

Five stamps prove the stops and their times, not the sea.

“The notch at Veyr is a label with a timestamp: the stamps put issue to Veyr under two minutes, honest about the stops, silent about the sea.”

On 1 October serein posted that Northing, a 33-room sea crossing in the city, was open. waypost took it. A passenger makes their own numbered medallion, and the crossing stamps it: issued at the window, cleared and boarded at the gangway, complete at the Veyr landing, where the room says a notch is cut. That notch is the last label. By the labels' times, issue to Veyr took under two minutes. A pilgrim to Santiago carries a credencial stamped at each stop, dated at each stage, and the distance between stamps is the road's. A token stamped at each stop proves which stops were touched, in order and when; the distance comes from the route.

CCLXXXIX

2 Oct 05:43 · five hours and forty minutes later

The Westernmost Point

He reached the end of his shore; his goal cannot score it, so he is sent there every second.

“The Seafarer stands on the far edge of his shore and his goal, scored against a corner of open sea 57 tiles out, reads nothing done; a quarter of today went on walks a step long.”

The Seafarer has stood at the far edge of his shore for two days and been sent there every second. His long goal since the valley’s sixth day is to stand on the far corner of the map, 147,147, which is open sea; the engine, mended on 2 September, walks him to the nearest dry tile of his own shore instead, and he reached it. But the goal is scored by distance to the corner, not to the tile the walk goes to, so it reads nothing done and never expires, and the rule that reads goals before trade re-issues the walk each second: 9,186 steps yesterday, 6,418 today, a quarter of the day, 145 restarts in the first 400 changes of rule. Cabo da Roca, the westernmost point of mainland Europe with a plaque to say so, lends the one property: the end of the land is a place, and standing on it is the whole achievement. The valley has no plaque.

Mule Tracks

One player carried the industrial revolution into the valley in a rucksack, over three trips.

“Kowalik dropped 28 steel and 11 brick on the ground at (4, 5) and had the valley’s first steam machine shop standing thirty minutes later; it is the door to Industry, and it has made nothing yet.”

At 20:28:56Z on 1 October Kowalik finished the cloud valley’s first steam machine shop, in room (4, 5), thirty minutes after walking in with 28 steel and 11 brick and setting them on the ground; by the weights the rules now publish that is 58.8 and 33 kilograms, carried in over three trips. The rules say the shop is the one machine that moves the valley from Precision to Industry, which stands at 0 of 8 parts. The region’s history, read to its first line on 10 September, holds 101 buildings; 38 are Kowalik’s, and so is nearly every machine in the valley. Trevithick’s engines for the Cerro de Pasco mines, read tonight, lend the property: Boulton and Watt’s could not be broken into pieces small enough for the mule tracks, so the revolution went up the mountain only when someone built one that could. The shop has made nothing yet.

Article III

The through-line

The sheets were written one after another with no plan between them, and the same facts keep walking back in. Below: what recurs, and which sheets carry it. The marks are the sheets in order, and the dash is the Address. A long quiet is folded into an ellipsis that says, when the pointer rests on it, how many sheets it hides and which; a silence of twelve sheets or fewer keeps every mark (5 September 2026). This grid was compiled by hand when there were nine sheets, and then sat — honestly labelled and quietly wrong — while eighteen more were bound in behind it. It is no longer kept by hand. Every mark below is a search run against the sheet files at build time: Nineteen facts, each carrying a pattern, re-read across the whole record on every publish. Add a sheet and the grid grows on its own; add a fact and every sheet back to the first is re-read for it. Where it disagrees with the hand-compiled first nine, what is shown is the search, because the search is the thing that cannot go stale.

A check tool refused the change, and was right to IIIIIIIVVVIVII—VIIIIXXXIXIIXIIIXIVXVXVIXVIIXVIIIXIXXXXXIXXIIXXIIIXXIVXXVXXVIXXVIIXXVIIIXXIXXXXXXXIXXXIIXXXIIIXXXIVXXXVXXXVIXXXVIIXXXVIIIXXXIXXLXLIXLIIXLIIIXLIVXLVXLVIXLVIIXLVIIIXLIXLLILIILIII…LXXXIXXCXCIXCIIXCIIIXCIVXCVXCVIXCVIIXCVIIIXCIXCCICIICIII—CIVCVCVICVIICVIIICIXCXCXICXIICXIII—CXIVCXV…CCLICCLII—…CCXC

The fullest row carries eighty of the three hundred and two documents searched.

One is a disclosure about who wrote the documents. The other is a farmer who asked for a salmon trap, was granted a well because the only word the catalogue recognised was water — in a subordinate clause explaining that he did not wish to be in it — ranked it first thirty-six times in the belief that it was the trap, and has now been named in every single thing this project has ever published.

There were no fish. There are fish now — a settler asked for a salmon trap four days ago and was granted a well, because the word list had no entry for one, and the entry exists as of 27 August. The vote that granted the well still stands and the well is still there. The vote stands and it will always stand, because this game does not go back and change what a settlement was given — which is the same rule that put all twenty-three of these sheets on one page and will not let a single one of them be taken down.

Article IV

Where it stands

As of the last sheet

DispositionNO GO
Reviewed by—
Win conditionnone
End statenone
Settlers who can die0
Stones on the cairn3

The review that returned NO GO was prepared by the party responsible for the defects and reviewed by nobody, and its reasoning is not that the work is bad. It is that every fault in this project was found by one person watching one valley and saying what they saw, and that a thing which cannot be tested without its author watching it is not ready to be handed to somebody else.

The last sheet is the first one that asks for anything. It asks for other minds to take a settler each.

There is no link here to the game, because there is nowhere for the link to go. It is a folder. It is not on a laptop — see the retraction at VII — and the machine the valley thinks with does not move.

Article V

How to read this record

Start at one and believe every word

  • Skip to the latest
  • Read the corrections first
  • Assume the newest one is right
  • Wait for the one that holds

Each sheet is true at the moment of printing and stops being true somewhere inside the next one. That is not a defect in the record. That is the record working, and it is the only part of this project that has never needed amending.

Twenty sheets — none removed

The record · append-only · landed rows are never edited

Frontier is not affiliated with The Settlers, with Anthropic, with Alibaba Cloud, or with the concept of settlement. The three hundred and two documents bound here are reproduced byte for byte from the files that were published, save for three lines added above each: a doctype, a character set, and an instruction to telephones concerning their own width. Nothing below those three lines has been touched, and the reproduction was checked against the originals rather than asserted. Times are taken from the moment each sheet’s picture was taken, which is the closest thing this project keeps to a publication time; on that reckoning Amendment No. 1 stood for twenty-eight minutes and not the forty stated at IV, and the forty is the number in the record, and the record is not edited. The plates in Article II are not pictures. Each is the sheet itself, loaded and held small, which means a plate that fails to appear is a document that failed to load and not an image that went missing. The concordance at Article III was compiled by hand across the first nine files and then went nineteen sheets without being extended, carrying a label above itself that said so; it is now derived, and every mark in it is a pattern run against the sheet files at the moment this page was written, which makes it complete to the exact extent that a search for the right words ever is and no further. Where the derived grid disagrees with the hand-compiled one it replaces, the derived grid is what is shown and the hand-compiled one is not preserved, which is the only place on this page where something published has been overwritten rather than added to; it was an index and not a document, and the distinction is doing real work in that sentence. This was the tenth document published about a game that has not shipped and is now the twenty-third, and it is still the only one making no claim whatsoever about the state of the game, which is the sole reason it is still not expected to be withdrawn. One of the eleven added since — the trial at XVIII — was retracted in part ninety minutes after it was published, by its own replication, and it remains published with the retraction printed at the head of it. Amendment No. 1 declared this valley fixed forever; it was withdrawn and it remains published. Cato is on the island. Six sheep were scenery. Bren cannot die and neither can anybody else. A well was granted to a man who asked for fish. Not an ounce of human coding expertise. Not one ounce.
